Nothing else is relaxed — `html:false` upstream still escapes all raw HTML in the source, no `ADD_ATTR`, no `ALLOW_UNKNOWN_PROTOCOLS`, and emphatically **not `annotation-xml`** (an HTML integration point and the seam behind DOMPurify's known MathML mXSS vectors; KaTeX never emits it). A unit test pins the config's exact WIDTH so growing it has to be a deliberate edit.
	- **Accepted narrowing, do NOT "fix" it by widening the allowlist:** the HTML parser re-namespaces an element nested in a MathML *text integration point* (`mi`/`mo`/`mn`/`ms`/`mtext`) into the HTML namespace, and DOMPurify then drops it if its name is a MathML tag — which happens for the `<mo><mrow>…` KaTeX emits for `\mathop{\rm lim}`-style constructs. It costs that construct its screen-reader text only; the visible rendering and the `<annotation>` TeX are intact, and ordinary `\lim`/`\operatorname`/`\sum` are unaffected. Recovering it means disabling exactly the mXSS guard that matters.
	- **CSS + fonts are BUNDLED, never a CDN.** `import 'katex/dist/katex.min.css'` sits in `markdown.ts` beside the engine (so "add a markdown surface" can't also mean "remember the math stylesheet"): Vite emits it into the app CSS and rewrites `url(fonts/KaTeX_*)` to hashed assets copied into `build/client/_app/immutable/assets/`, with the one font under the 4 KB inline limit riding along as a `data:` URI — offline-safe, CSP-tight, verified in the PACKAGED build. `app.css` adds only four `.cellar-md` rules (size, display-math horizontal scroll, block margin, error node); KaTeX sets no color, so math tracks `base-content` in both themes for free. **`katex` is a TOP-LEVEL dependency (`^0.16.47`) for that import, and its range must stay compatible with the plugin's own (`^0.16.4`)**: the stylesheet has to belong to the very katex the plugin typesets with. Both ranges resolve to ONE hoisted copy today; a bump that split them into two installed versions would ship a CSS/JS pair disagreeing about class names and font metrics - which surfaces as broken glyphs, not as an install error.
	- **The plugin is CommonJS and the two builds interop with it DIFFERENTLY.** Vite bundles it for the browser and yields the `.default` function; SvelteKit leaves it **external** in the server build, where Node's own interop yields the whole `module.exports` (`{default: fn}`). `markdown.ts` normalizes that. Skip it and `md.use()` throws `plugin.apply is not a function` at module load, so **every SSR page 500s and the app never boots** — a failure the Vite-bundled unit suite structurally cannot see. That is why `tests/e2e/markdown-math.spec.ts` boots the REAL packaged launcher; it also asserts a KaTeX font actually LOADED (not merely referenced) and that no request left Cellar's origin.
	- **HTML export is deliberately NOT math-aware.** `export-html.ts` keeps its own markdown-it (it always did — it is server-side and needs no DOMPurify), so an exported notebook shows `$…$` as literal, legible text. Making it typeset would mean inlining KaTeX's CSS + ~1 MB of fonts into every exported file; the cheap future move is KaTeX `output:'mathml'` there (native browser MathML, zero assets), deliberately not done here.
	- **Math is ONE OPAQUE UNIT to find-in-page, and that is what keeps the active match honest** (`domHighlight.ts`'s `MATH_SELECTOR` + `mathSourceText`). KaTeX writes each expression's text into the container THREE times - the clipped screen-reader `.katex-mathml` MathML, its `<annotation>` raw TeX, and the visible aria-hidden `.katex-html` glyphs - while the search engine counts the expression ONCE from the cell source (`strippedMarkdown` does not strip `$…$`). A plain text walk therefore counted three occurrences per expression, so the model's ordinal slid onto an invisible node (Ctrl+F emphasized a 1px clipped MathML node and the painted count diverged from the reported one). `buildTextRanges` now REPLACES a `.katex`/`.katex-error` subtree with the expression's own source TeX between its delimiters - the same string the model counted - and returns a **POSITIONAL** array whose entry is `null` where an occurrence cannot be painted (inside math: the glyphs are a different string from the source). Callers index by ordinal and must tolerate the hole; compacting it slides every later ordinal, which is the bug. What must be preserved is the SEQUENCE of occurrences, not the region's length - so the substitution reads what the SOURCE wrote, never what KaTeX chose to render, in two places that are easy to get backwards. **The delimiter comes from the plugin's `<p class="katex-block">` wrapper alone**, which marks a `$$…$$` source and nothing else: the plugin promotes inline `\begin{align|equation|gather|cd|alignat}` math to `displayMode`, so `.katex-display` appears over a single-`$` source and keying off it rebuilt `$$…$$`, inventing two `$` per side. **And TWO different nodes wear `katex-error`, carrying the TeX in OPPOSITE places** - KaTeX's own (`renderError`, the `throwOnError:false` `ParseError` swallow) puts the expression in the visible text and the message in `title`, while the markdown-it plugin's own `catch` (reachable because `throwOnError` suppresses only `ParseError`, so a `RangeError` from deeply nested input still propagates) puts the latex in `title` and the message in the text. They are told apart STRUCTURALLY (`isKatexNativeError`: KaTeX always builds a `<span>` and sets `title`+`style="color:…"` on the same three lines; the plugin sets no `style` and its block form is a `<p>`, a shape KaTeX never emits), never by guessing which string looks like a message. Correspondence is exact up to `strippedMarkdown`'s own light syntax stripping - which is also why the `` $`x`$ `` form reconstructs WITHOUT its backticks (the model's stripped text has none either) - plus two accepted narrowings: a fenced `$$` block loses the newline after its opening delimiter, so only a query spanning that newline is affected; and `strippedMarkdown` collapses `_x_`/`*x*`/`~~x~~` pairs across the WHOLE source, math included, while the walk rebuilds the TeX verbatim, so a query matching a bare emphasis character drifts (`Use $x_1$ and $y_1$; snake_case names.` = one `_` in the model, three in the walk). That one changes the occurrence COUNT, not just a length, and is left alone on purpose - `strippedMarkdown` is the engine's cached hot path and is deliberately math-unaware, while emphasis-collapsing the TeX instead is simply wrong (`x_1` is a subscript). A `.katex` subtree with NO `<annotation>` contributes the DELIMITERS ALONE, never the `.katex-html` glyphs (a different string ⇒ shifted ordinals); unreachable unless someone sets `output:'html'`/`'mathml'` in `MATH_OPTIONS` or drops `annotation` from the sanitizer allowlist. Painting inside math is the deliberate cost of that exactness.
	- Covered by `tests/unit/markdown-math.test.ts` (jsdom — the sanitizer is half the feature, so the tests assert DOMPurify's real behavior, not the shape of its config; plus the size bound, the math-free output renderer, and the code-span exemption; **`jsdom` is pinned to a major whose own `engines.node` is `>=18`, the floor `package.json` declares - bumping it past that means bumping CI's Node (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`, pinned to LTS 20) FIRST**: jsdom 30 requires Node `^22.22.2 || ^24.15.0 || >=26` and throws at IMPORT on anything older, and that does not fail honestly - the jsdom files never load, so vitest prints every OTHER file green beside an unhandled error and only the exit code disagrees), `tests/unit/search-math-highlight.test.ts` (the model→DOM ordinal correspondence, asserted per ordinal - "some highlight exists" would not have caught it), `tests/e2e/markdown-math.spec.ts` and the math case in `tests/e2e/find-highlight.spec.ts` (real browser: the painted count equals the reported one and no active emphasis ever sits inside a `.katex` subtree).
- **MCP agent interface.** `src/lib/server/mcp/` — the agent surface (spec §4). `service.js` = transport-independent tool logic (section tree from markdown headers, tiered/summarized outputs, visibility filtering, UUID-addressed read/write/execute); `server.js` = `McpServer` + Streamable HTTP transport on its own port (`CELLAR_MCP_PORT`, default 39587 but the launcher now allocates a **free** port per instance so concurrent `cellar` runs don't collide, path `/mcp`), started once from `src/hooks.server.js`. Runs **in-process** in the SvelteKit backend so it shares the live `notebook.js` doc + `kernel.js` — restarting the kernel can't drop the MCP session/document (the #1 requirement). Per-cell hide/show via `metadata.cellar.hidden_from_agent` (allowlisted, visible by default) is honored in every map/read/search/section/result. Kernel service methods `restartKernel/interruptKernel/kernelStatus` live in `kernel.js`; richer read/write (`listCells/getCell/setVisibility/moveCellTo`) in `notebook.js`. **Coherence doctrine:** `server.js` passes an `INSTRUCTIONS` house-style string as the `McpServer` `instructions` (advisory, delivered once at connect: imports-at-top convention, check `kernel_state` first, continue the story via `get_notebook_map`, structure with markdown) + a matching `cellar_notebook_style` prompt. The read-group **`kernel_state`** tool returns the live namespace bucketed into imports/functions/classes/variables (types, shapes, dataframe columns); it reuses `inspect.js`'s shared probe (`kernelState()`), short-circuits to `{started:false}` when `kernelStatus()` is `not_started` (no force-boot) and to `{started:true,busy:true}` when the kernel is busy (never queues a probe behind a running cell), and dedupes imports by statement. Its `session_id` is the epoch the **probe itself executed in** (taken from `execute()`'s `kernel` event, never sampled around the `await`), so it always identifies the very session the returned namespace was read from; a restart between probe and reply yields `stale:true` rather than silently pairing an old epoch with a new namespace. `inspectVariables()` (sidebar) shares that probe but projects to the original `{name,type,shape,preview}` shape. **Live vs persisted execution (agent-safety invariant).** A cell's `outputs` live in the `.ipynb` and outlive the kernel *and* the server process, so they can NEVER answer "did this run in the kernel I'm talking to" — conflating the two let an agent trust a saved `run_status:"ok"` and build on variables that were never defined this session (→ `NameError`). `kernel.js` owns a monotonic **session epoch** (`kernelSession()`/`currentSessionId()`), bumped on first start, `restartKernel()`, `rebindKernel()`, and jupyter's own `autorestarting`. The epoch bumps on the **failure** paths too (a `finally` in `restartKernel()`, before the replacement start in `rebindKernel()`): once the REST restart is issued the namespace is gone even if the websocket reconnect rejects, and the epoch is monotonic + opaque, so over-invalidating is always the safe direction while a missing bump re-opens the `NameError` trap. The `autorestarting` handler is identity-guarded (`currentKernel === kernel`) so a late emission from an **outgoing** kernel cannot bump the epoch of the one that replaced it, and it re-injects `initKernel()` for parity with `restartKernel()` (an autorestart clears the namespace *and* the matplotlib backend); `rebindKernel()` disconnects the handler on teardown. Both run entry points (UI `/run` route + MCP `runCell`) stamp the epoch the run **started** in onto the runtime-only `metadata.cellar.lastRun` — captured from `execute()`'s `kernel` event and **never re-read via `currentSessionId()` on the error path**, since an autorestart mid-run bumps the epoch and would otherwise stamp the *new* one, marking a cell that never touched the fresh namespace as live (it stays null when no kernel ever started, so `ran_this_session` is false). The stamp is runtime-only in **both** directions, through one shared helper (`clean.js`'s exported `stripRuntimeMeta`, which strips every runtime-only `cellar` key - `lastRun`, `editedAt`, `importBindings` - and whose two halves must stay in lockstep): `clean.js`'s `cleanCell` strips `lastRun` before disk (**never persist the epoch**) and `ipynb.js`'s `deserialize()` strips it on read, so a hand-edited or externally-authored `.ipynb` cannot forge `ok_session` (epochs are small monotonic ints, so a literal `session: 1` would otherwise match the first real kernel). The stamp may only ever originate from an in-process run. `service.js` derives `ran_this_session` + a split `run_status` (`ok_session`/`error_session` = executed against the live namespace; `ok_persisted`/`error_persisted` = saved output from a prior session; `unrun`; `error_kernel_unavailable`) from that stamp, never from `outputs.length` — a cell can run successfully and emit nothing (`lp = load()`). It checks the recorded status **positively** (`=== 'ok'`), so jupyter's `abort` reply degrades to `error_session` rather than a false `ok_session`. The epoch-equality rule lives in exactly one predicate (`isLiveSession`, wrapped by `ranThisSession`) - never re-derive it inline. **`error_kernel_unavailable`** covers the third case: `execute()` threw before emitting its `kernel` event (sidecar unreachable), so no session exists to stamp and the synthesized `CellarError` would otherwise fall through to `error_persisted` - telling the agent to ignore, as leftover, a failure the run it just requested raised seconds ago. Both run entry points record a `kernel_unavailable:true` marker on `lastRun` (runtime-only, so it rides the same strip-on-read/write guard); `runStatus` and `get_errors` surface it, but **only while the kernel is still unreachable** (`sid == null`) - the marker is *derived*, never trusted as sticky truth. That gate is load-bearing: the boot race (MCP serves before the sidecar finishes starting, the first `run_cell` is rejected, the sidecar comes up moments later) would otherwise leave one cell claiming a LIVE kernel-down failure forever, since only re-running *that* cell clears the flag - the same mislabel with the sign flipped. Once a kernel exists the cell falls back to `error_persisted`, which is honest. It is a status about the **run attempt** and is orthogonal to `ran_this_session`, which stays false - nothing executed in any session. A multi-cell read (`readCells`/`readSection`) samples the epoch **once** per response, so a restart landing mid-loop can't classify one payload against two sessions. **Every** read tool that surfaces saved outputs carries `ran_this_session`, `get_full_output` and `search_cells` included, so no tool hands an agent a stale output with no live/persisted signal; `get_notebook_map` carries a `kernel` header and `get_errors` flags stale errors. `execute()`'s `internal:true` opt-out keeps `inspect.js`'s probes out of `execs_this_session`. Note the deliberate scope: `ran_this_session` means "this cell executed in this epoch", **not** "the cell's current source ran" — editing after a run does not clear the stamp.
- **Figures reach the agent as REAL images, not a placeholder (`src/lib/server/mcp/image.ts`).** An image cell-output (a matplotlib figure) is returned to an MCP agent as an actual **image content block** (`{type:'image', data, mimeType}`), so the agent can SEE the chart it just drew. Before, every path returned only the text marker `[image/png, 978×536, 44 KB]` and an agent authoring plots was blind to its own work - the reported workaround was a throwaway `fig.savefig()` cell, `Read` the PNG, delete the cell. **The split (deliberate, it is the cost policy):** the **run** path (`run_cell`, and therefore `add_and_run` - the single round-trip write-run-see loop) and **`get_full_output`** ship images; the **scan** paths (`get_notebook_map`, `read_cell(s)`, `read_by_location`, `read_section`, `search_cells`, `get_errors`) keep the enriched, image-token-free placeholder (`imagePlaceholder`) - a read over 40 cells must not dump 40 figures. A **batch** run (`run_cells`/`run_all`/`run_stale`/`run_range`) does not inline either (its records are deliberately compact): each record carries **`has_image:true`** and the figure is one `get_full_output(id)` away; `runCell`'s `skipImages` opt-out is what keeps a batch from decoding+resampling N rasters only to discard them. **`buildImageBlocks` in `image.ts` is the ONE policy seam** - every tool that ships images goes through it, so the bound cannot drift: PNG downscaled to `IMG_MAX_EDGE` (768px longest edge, ~1600 image tokens → ~590; `size:'full'` still returns the ORIGINAL bytes), at most `MAX_IMAGE_BLOCKS` (4) per **automatic run** result, and a decoded-byte/per-edge SHIP ceiling (`IMAGE_BLOCK_MAX_BYTES`, `MAX_IMAGE_EDGE_HARD`) checked by `withinHostLimits` on the raster actually EMITTED, so an oversized `full` image is downscaled to FIT rather than shipped. Nothing is dropped silently: whatever is declined is reported in `images_omitted` with a reason + the route to it - and **that route is real, which is why the 4-cap is caller-scoped**: `getFullOutput` (an EXPLICIT per-cell request) ships up to `MAX_FULL_OUTPUT_IMAGE_BLOCKS` (20), far past the run result's four, so the `limit` note is not a promise nothing keeps. **But every bound stays FINITE - an earlier `limit: Infinity` there was wrong** and must not come back: each image over `IMG_MAX_EDGE` is a synchronous decode + resample + re-encode on the shared event loop that also streams SSE frames and services every other kernel and MCP session, so an unbounded pass over a cell that plotted in a loop (30-50 figures is ordinary) stalls the whole process for seconds and, on `size:'full'`, returns tens of MB. Two AGGREGATE budgets bound one result alongside the count: `IMAGE_RESULT_MAX_BYTES` (8 MB of shipped raster - the per-image `IMAGE_BLOCK_MAX_BYTES` bounds ONE, nothing else bounded N) and `MAX_RESULT_DECODE_PIXELS` (40 MP of SOURCE pixels handed to the decoder - the CPU sibling, since a downscaled output's size says nothing about what it cost to produce; charged BEFORE the decode whenever the cost is knowable, so a decode that would blow the budget never runs, and charged even when the block is then declined on bytes). **NO path may decode uncharged, and the two leaks that made that untrue are both closed:** a raster already inside `maxEdge` **on dimensions the caller already parsed never reaches `PNG.sync.read` at all** (`downscaleImageForBlock` early-returns those dims - decoding a whole image merely to re-read a width and height it was handed allocates the w×h×4 buffer and discards it, and `willDecode` reported that as no-decode, so it was free); and a raster whose header would NOT parse (`dim === null`, which nothing upstream can pre-charge and `exceedsDecodeBound` cannot refuse) is charged AFTER the fact from the raster pngjs really read. `ScaledImage.decodedPixels` is **required, not optional** - every return path has to STATE its cost, so a future path cannot quietly omit it - and `buildImageBlocks` refuses to even START such an unpredictable decode once the budget is spent, which bounds the overshoot to a single image. **That refusal is gated on a decode really being about to happen, through `willDecode` on BOTH paths** (which therefore reports an unparseable-header PNG on the DEFAULT path as a decode - `downscaleImageForBlock` skips both early returns without dims and does reach `PNG.sync.read`; a predicate that is false exactly where a decode happens is a landmine in a module whose invariant is that none goes uncharged) - only a PNG is ever handed to pngjs, so an unreadable-header JPEG/GIF, or a `full` PNG already inside the host ceilings, costs the decoder NOTHING; withholding one on a spent pixel budget charged a figure for CPU that was never going to be spent, which is the mirror-image error of an uncharged decode. **The pixel budget is charged on the `size:'full'` path too, and that is not a detail** - `full` skips the ROUTINE downscale, but `fitImageBlock` still falls back to a real decode+resample whenever a full-size original breaches a host ceiling, and that fallback emits a SMALL block, so the byte budget cannot see it either. `willDecode` therefore asks "will this decode", not "is this the default path" (`!withinHostLimits(...)` on the `full` branch); without it one `get_full_output(size:'full')` over a cell of oversized figures ran up to 20 decodes of up to 32 MP each, entirely uncharged - exactly the event-loop stall the budget exists to bound. **What makes all three capability-free is the ROUTE, not lifting them**: every `limit`/`budget` omission names `get_full_output(id, images_from: <that output_index>)`, a real param that resumes at exactly the figure which did not fit - so a hundred-figure cell is PAGED, never truncated. **A CONSECUTIVE run declined by the SAME aggregate bound collapses into ONE `images_omitted` entry** carrying `count` + that resume index, because a `limit`/`budget` decline is one bound hit once and says nothing about the individual figure: 60 loop-plotted figures used to return 4 blocks and 56 near-identical ~105-char notes (~8 KB) on the very path the 4-cap exists to keep cheap, and because an `images_from` page re-emits the shrinking tail, the omission text over a full page-through was QUADRATIC in the number of figures. The nothing-declined-silently contract still holds literally - the collapsed entry says HOW MANY were declined and names the call that resumes at the first of them. **`count` means CONSECUTIVE outputs, so the collapse is tracked by an `openBound` entry that ANY other outcome clears - never by "the last omission has the same reason"**: both budgets are `spent + cost > max` tests, not latches, so a large figure can be declined and a smaller FOLLOWING one still fit and ship; merging across that shipped image would make one entry claim a run that COVERED an output the result actually delivered, which is the very promise the agent pages on. A per-figure reason (`unsupported_mime`, `too_large`) names a specific figure AND a specific cause, so it is never merged - and it breaks a run too, since the images either side of it were declined for unrelated reasons. **The pixel bound is checked BEFORE any decode (`MAX_DECODE_PIXELS`, 32 MP, via the header-only `exceedsDecodeBound`), and that ordering is the point** - `PNG.sync.read` allocates width×height×4 up front, so one pathological figure (`plt.figure(figsize=(100,100), dpi=300)` → 30000×30000) would ask pngjs for gigabytes, and a genuine OOM is not catchable: it kills the single shared Node process, taking every notebook's kernel websocket, every SSE stream and every other agent session with it. Such a raster is omitted `too_large` and keeps its text placeholder, never decoded (the guard sits in BOTH `fitImageBlock` and `downscaleImageForBlock`, so no future caller can route around it). **That pre-decode refusal is TOTAL PIXELS ONLY, never the per-edge `MAX_IMAGE_EDGE_HARD`** - `w×h×4` is the entire allocation risk, and a single long edge costs nothing to decode: a stacked-subplot / FacetGrid figure (`plt.subplots(30,1,figsize=(8,60),dpi=150)` → 1200×9000, ~11 MP) is past the edge ceiling yet far under 32 MP, and MUST be decoded, downscaled to 768px and shipped. Refusing it pre-decode returned a placeholder for a figure that fits perfectly - do not re-conflate the two bounds. The edge ceiling is enforced where it belongs, on the EMITTED raster (`withinHostLimits`), which a downscale always satisfies; a non-PNG cannot be resampled, so an over-edge JPEG/GIF is still declined there. **`INLINE_IMAGE_MIMES` is an ALLOWLIST, and that is load-bearing** - an LLM host accepts a bounded raster set and REJECTS the whole tool result on anything else, so an `image/svg+xml` figure (matplotlib's svg backend) must keep its text placeholder or it would fail the very call that drew it. **`image/webp` is deliberately OUT** for the sibling reason: the module ships only a raster it has VERIFIED is inside the ceilings, and `imageDimensions` has no webp parser, so an oversized webp would pass the dimension check vacuously (and could not be resampled to fit) - re-adding it means adding a VP8/VP8L/VP8X header read first. Same reason `blockMime()` normalizes the unregistered `image/jpg` bundle key to `image/jpeg` when BUILDING the block: the output is still shown, but the emitted `mimeType` must be a type the host accepts. When ONE bundle carries several rasterizations of a figure (matplotlib `figure_formats={'png','svg'}` emits both), `service.ts`'s `imageKey` prefers an INLINABLE key over the merely-first one, so the agent gets the PNG rather than an `unsupported_mime` decline - and because the placeholder derives from that same function, `images[i].mime` and `outputs[i].image` can never disagree for one `output_index`. `has_image` on a batch record is decided by the policy's own header-only predicate **`canInlineImage`** (not merely by the mime), so the flag means what its description promises - a figure `get_full_output` can actually SHOW, never a round trip that returns the same placeholder. **A cell hidden from the agent ships NO image** - `runCell` gates `images` on `isHidden(cell)` beside the existing `hidden:true` annotation, because a picture reveals far more than the outputs TEXT a hidden cell has always returned (that text is pre-existing behavior and stays; this must not widen what `hidden_from_agent` discloses), and `getFullOutput` refuses a hidden cell outright - so without the gate the two tools disagreed about what the flag withholds. Whether a hidden cell may RUN is unchanged, and the batch path already skips hidden cells entirely. **Layering:** `service.ts` (transport-independent) decides WHICH images ship and at what size and returns them on `result.images`; `server.ts`'s `textWithImages` lifts the base64 out of the JSON text into image blocks - never both, or the same raster is billed twice. Every output still appears in `outputs` with its placeholder, and each image names its `output_index`, so the agent knows which output the picture is. **Two cost rules on the way there, both load-bearing because they run for every image of every result:** the base64 payload is read with `asText`, NEVER `String()` (nbformat stores a raster as an ARRAY of lines, and `String(['ab','cd'])` comma-joins it into base64 a strict host validator rejects - failing the ENTIRE tool result, the exact failure the ceilings exist to prevent) **and the emitted payload is whitespace-stripped once at the BUILD site** (`base64Payload` in `fitImageBlock`'s `build`, not in any one branch): nbformat splits with `splitlines(True)`, so the line terminators SURVIVE that join, and a terminator is the same class of non-alphabet character as the comma - every non-re-encoding path (`size:'full'`, any JPEG/GIF, a PNG already inside `maxEdge` - the common 640×480 matplotlib default - and the decode-failure fallback) ships the payload verbatim, which is why the strip belongs where the block is built. It copies, unlike `base64Bytes` which counts in place, and that is affordable for exactly that reason: at most `limit` blocks per result, versus several times per image; and `imageDimensions` decodes only a bounded PREFIX of the base64 (`HEADER_B64_CHARS`, with a larger window for JPEG, whose SOF sits past the APPn segments) rather than the whole raster - allocating megabytes to read a 24-byte header, several times per image, is what made the "header-only, costs nothing" claim false. `base64Bytes` counts in place for the same reason (a `replace(/\s+/g,'')` copied the whole payload). Parsed dims are THREADED through `exceedsDecodeBound`/`downscaleImageForBlock`/`withinHostLimits`/`fitImageBlock` instead of re-derived at each, and they are also the FALLBACK for a block's own `width`/`height`: those used to be a side effect of the downscale decode, so a `size:'full'` block and every JPEG/GIF carried no pixel size while a downscaled one did - metadata we already had in scope. `clean.ts` is untouched: this is a read-time projection, so the `.ipynb` and the UI render are unaffected. Covered by `tests/unit/mcp-image-blocks.test.ts` (policy + transport) + `tests/unit/mcp-image-downscale.test.ts` (downscale + the default-vs-full contract) + `tests/e2e/mcp-agent-sees-figures.spec.ts` (real matplotlib → `add_and_run` over a real `cellar mcp` bridge → a viewable image block, with the human's browser still rendering the same figure).
- **Agent-ergonomics conventions on the MCP surface (handles everywhere, batch writes, bounded inspection, cheap schemas).** Four rules that came out of real agent feedback and now bind every future tool. (1) **Every cell reference is a HANDLE, including a DESTINATION.** `move_cell` used to take only an absolute `position`, so an agent had to fetch `get_notebook_map` just to learn an index for a move it could already name; it now takes `after_id`/`before_id` (`position` still works and means *the index the cell ends up at*, which is exactly what `moveCellTo` wants). The off-by-one lives in ONE place, `service.ts`'s `destIndex`: `moveCellTo` inserts into the array AFTER the cell is lifted out, so an anchor BELOW the moved cell shifts up by one - do not re-derive that at a call site. An anchor equal to the moved cell is refused (`same_cell`), never silently treated as a move. The anchor goes through `asFullId` in the service as well as `resolveOne` at the boundary, keeping the documented symmetry that the ids this layer EMITS can be fed straight back into the ids it ACCEPTS. (2) **A repeated write gets a BATCH tool, not a loop of calls** - `delete_cell` became **`delete_cells(ids)`** (mirroring `read_cells`: a single-element array deletes one cell, so there is one tool, not two schemas). Addressing is **all-or-nothing** - every ref resolves before anything is deleted, so a typo in the fifth handle cannot leave the first four gone - duplicates collapse, and the whole batch takes **ONE** `autoCheckpointBeforeAgentAction`, so the human's undo restores the state before the pivot rather than after seven eighths of it. A batch that would delete EVERY cell is **refused** by the shared non-empty invariant (`deleteCells`), and `removeCells` looks that refusal up through `deleteWouldEmptyNotebook` BEFORE checkpointing, so a refused batch mints no snapshot - see the Multi-cell selection entry. **`clear_outputs(ids?)` is the second application of that rule** (`server.ts` registration -> `service.ts` `clearOutputs` -> `notebook.ts` `clearOutputsForCells`): same handle addressing, same all-or-nothing resolve, same ONE checkpoint, and - like `deleteCells` and for the same reason - ONE document write plus one `cell:cleared` per cell, never a loop over the single-cell `clearOutputs` (which persists the whole `.ipynb` per cell; a 300-cell clear-all would be 300 whole-file writes). Its ADDRESSING rule is the one thing a future edit must not blur: **omitting `ids` clears EVERY cell, an explicitly EMPTY array is a REFUSAL** - an agent whose computed id list came out empty must never wipe the notebook by accident, mirroring `delete_cells`' empty-batch refusal. Only cells that HAVE outputs are touched (the UI `clearAll` guards the same way), so an output-less cell is a genuine no-op: not listed in `cleared`, no event, and a batch that would change nothing takes no checkpoint and writes nothing. **`lastRun` is deliberately untouched**, matching the UI clear exactly: `run_status`/`ran_this_session`/staleness derive from the run stamp and NEVER from `outputs.length`, so clearing output flips only `has_output` - do not "fix" this by also clearing the stamp. **Three further rules govern what the RESULT may CLAIM.** (a) A cell whose run is IN FLIGHT is **skipped, never cleared**, and named in `skipped`: the run's next accumulator flush (`setOutputsLive`) and its `run:end` (`setOutputs`) write the outputs straight back, so clearing it would report a success the notebook never keeps. "In flight" is read from the **run queue's `running` entry** for that notebook (`queueStateFor`) - the in-memory outputs cannot answer it, since `setOutputsLive` keeps them current mid-stream and a mid-run buffer is indistinguishable from a settled one - and the skip is checked BEFORE the has-outputs guard, since it is about the cell being in the kernel's hands, not about whether its buffer happens to be empty right now. A merely QUEUED cell has emitted nothing yet, so clearing it is honest and it is NOT skipped. (b) The **clear-all form filters a cell hidden from the agent out of `cleared`/`count`/`skipped`** while still clearing it: it addresses cells the agent never named, so returning a hidden handle would disclose both the cell's existence and its address, which `hidden_from_agent` ("honored in every map, read, search, section and result") forbids - `run_all` draws exactly this line (it RUNS hidden cells, it just omits them from results), and `count` is `cleared.length` AFTER the filter so the two cannot disagree. An EXPLICIT id list is deliberately NOT filtered - those handles came from the agent, so echoing one back discloses nothing new, the same reason `delete_cells` reports named cells. (c) **UNDO IS NOT PROMISED, because the shared checkpoint rules cannot keep that promise for THIS tool** - `autoCheckpointBeforeAgentAction` is THROTTLED (one snapshot per `CHECKPOINT_EVERY_N_ACTIONS`, so a clear is often folded into a batch protected by an earlier snapshot) and a snapshot past `MAX_SNAPSHOT_BYTES` keeps sources but DROPS every cell's outputs - which is precisely the output-heavy notebook this tool exists to shed weight from, and the cleared document is persisted straight afterwards, so those outputs are gone for good. The tool description therefore says undo MAY NOT restore them (never "one undoable checkpoint"), and the result carries **`undo: {outputs_recoverable:false, reason}`** whenever the checkpoint the service just took cannot give them back - read off the `CheckpointMeta` that call already returns, so it changes no checkpoint behavior. **`delete_cells` is deliberately NOT the same case** (its checkpoint's value is the cell SOURCES, which survive truncation), and both the flag and the description's honesty are pinned by tests so the over-claim cannot regrow. Finally `clearOutputsForCells` guards its persist with `doc.jpFormat` exactly as `setOutputs` twenty lines above it does - and the SINGLE-cell `clearOutputs` the UI drives carries the same guard, so which surface the human used cannot decide what the disk does: a `.py` notebook carries no outputs on disk, so the write would be a blocking `spawnSync` jupytext conversion producing byte-identical bytes and churning mtime - the `cell:cleared` EVENTS still fire unconditionally, so every open tab clears. Covered by `tests/unit/mcp-clear-outputs.test.ts`. (1), (2) and `clear_outputs` are covered by `tests/unit/mcp-move-delete-ergonomics.test.ts` / `tests/unit/mcp-clear-outputs.test.ts` (real service + notebook: move by handle with the `destIndex` off-by-one and the `same_cell`/anchor-both refusals, batch delete all-or-nothing + one checkpoint, clear by handle + clear-all + the empty-list refusal + the `lastRun` invariant + the running-cell skip against a REAL queue ticket + the hidden-cell filter in both directions + a `.py` doc clearing with no jupytext write + the `undo` warning present on a THROTTLED-away and on a size-TRUNCATED checkpoint and ABSENT on one that really holds the outputs + the description's honesty and its length bound) alongside `tests/e2e/mcp-ergonomics.spec.ts` (real wire). (3) **`inspect_variable` is bounded on every axis a value can grow along, and SAYS which bound bit.** A row count alone does not bound a head: ten rows of a 1024-float embedding column is ten thousand numbers (the reported bug - one call cost thousands of tokens). So `inspect.ts` exports `INSPECT_HEAD_ROWS`/`INSPECT_ARRAY_HEAD_ROWS`/`INSPECT_ARRAY_ITEMS`/`INSPECT_STR_CHARS`/`INSPECT_HEAD_BUDGET`/`INSPECT_ARRAY_REPR_CHARS` and interpolates them into BOTH the python probe and the tool description, so prose and policy cannot drift (the `IMAGE_DOC` precedent). The probe's `_cellar_arrayish` decides the row cap **before** serializing (`to_json` on ten array rows materializes the very payload being bounded) and `_cellar_shrink` bounds each value; the shrink is applied **per RECORD, never over the record list** - shrinking the outer list would cap the ROW count at `INSPECT_ARRAY_ITEMS` and quietly shorten an ordinary scalar head. A dict keeps EVERY key (a spent budget yields `…`, never a missing column - an absent column reads as a frame that does not have it), an array truncation carries `… N more (M total)`, and `head_truncated` + `head_note` name only the bounds that ACTUALLY bit (a two-row frame of arrays is not "capped at 3 rows"). Once the head is bounded the `repr` is cut to `INSPECT_ARRAY_REPR_CHARS` too - it is the same arrays a second time. Scalar variables are untouched. `list_variables` needs none of this (no head; `repr_short` is already 140 chars). Covered by `tests/unit/inspect-variable-array-cap.test.ts` (real probe; the pandas cases `describe.skipIf` with the reason IN THE SUITE NAME) + `tests/e2e/mcp-ergonomics.spec.ts` (real wire, real kernel, pandas installed into the workspace venv). (4) **A tool description is paid on EVERY session, so it is a cost, not free documentation** - ~51 tools, and the schemas load before the first call. Keep them concise; hedging that restates the same caveat twice is the thing to cut. **But the honesty claims are NOT padding and are pinned by tests** (`tests/unit/symbol-graph.test.ts`): `cell_impact` and `find_symbol` must keep saying that the graph under-reports, that **NOTHING catches it at run time** (`stale_state` is the SAME static graph plus run timestamps and never inspects the kernel namespace), and what to do instead. Trim the wording, never the claim - the false "authoritative post-hoc signal" backstop is exactly what those guards exist to stop coming back. **`clear_outputs` is the first description carrying a MECHANICAL bound** (**under 700 chars**), which is how those two halves are reconciled: an honesty correction has to be paid for by cutting words elsewhere rather than by growing the string every session pays for. It is asserted in **BOTH** places on purpose - over the real wire in `tests/e2e/mcp-ergonomics.spec.ts` (the length an agent is actually billed for) **and** on the registration literal in `tests/unit/mcp-clear-outputs.test.ts`, because **e2e is deliberately absent from CI and the no-mistakes gate**, so a wire-only assertion would let an over-grown description merge green and fail much later. The unit half reads the source, so it needs the registration's `description:` to stay a **single-quoted literal on one line**; reformatting it to a template literal or wrapping it breaks that test rather than the bound. **The nbdev export PAIR carries that same bound** - `set_cell_export` (`tests/unit/mcp-set-cell-export.test.ts`) and `set_export_target` (`tests/unit/mcp-export-target.test.ts`), each pinned on its registration literal: a run of honesty corrections had grown `set_export_target` to 913 chars by restating the `.py`-text-notebook refusal as "like its pair" and re-stating what `get_notebook_map`'s own description says about the reported value, so the rule is applied where the growth actually happened. Trim the RESTATEMENTS, never the claims - each test also pins the specific claims an agent acts on, so cutting one fails loudly.
- **Per-MCP-session working notebook (multi-agent targeting).** The captain runs many agents against ONE Cellar, each on its own notebook. Every MCP tool used to resolve to the single global **active** notebook (`getActiveNotebookPath()`), which is *also* the user's focused tab - so two agents couldn't work two notebooks, and the user switching tabs redirected every agent. Now each MCP **session** (one per connected `cellar mcp` stdio bridge, keyed by its Streamable-HTTP `Mcp-Session-Id`, surfaced to every tool handler as **`extra.sessionId`**) may PIN its own working notebook. `service.ts` holds `sessionNotebooks: Map<sessionId, absPath>`; **`targetFor(sessionId, explicit?)`** resolves a call's target as **explicit per-call `notebook` > this session's pin > global active** (the last is the backward-compatible fallback for a lone agent that never pins - single-agent behavior is unchanged). `server.ts` computes `targetOf(extra, notebook)` once per tool and threads it as the `nb` arg into the (already per-notebook) `notebook.ts`/service ops - every read/write/run/checkpoint tool now carries an optional **`notebook`** param for a one-off cross-notebook op. **`use_notebook(name)`** (open-or-create) is the declare-your-notebook tool; `open_notebook`/`create_notebook` also pin the calling session; `current_notebook` reports the pin vs the active fallback; `list_notebooks` flags each entry `working` (this session) vs `active` (the user's tab). **Focus decoupling:** agent open/create/pin surface the notebook via `notebook:opened` with **`focus:false`** - `activateAndBroadcast(abs, originId, {focus})` in `notebook.ts` skips setting `activePath` (the user-focus proxy) when `focus:false`, and `+page.svelte`'s `surfaceFilePermanent` opens it as an AVAILABLE (non-active) tab without stealing the user's focus (it only takes focus when the user has no active tab to lose). The user's own open/create (`{create:true}` REST route, cross-tab) still uses `focus:true`. So switching the user's tab never redirects an agent, and an agent editing never yanks the user. INSTRUCTIONS clause 6 ("declare your working notebook first") + clause 8 (shared kernel across agents) updated; agent-facing descriptions say "your working notebook", not "the active notebook". **A `.py` notebook is pinnable and listable too, and that is a correctness rule rather than a convenience** - `use_notebook` is the ONLY defense against the target following the USER'S focused tab, so an agent that cannot pin has its writes silently redirected mid-task (observed: three cells ran in a `.py`, the user switched tabs, later writes addressed another notebook). `notebookNameToPath` takes a path that NAMES a notebook FORMAT (`.ipynb`, `.py`) literally and appends `.ipynb` to **everything else, whatever it is and whether or not a file exists there** - mere existence must never make a path notebook-shaped, since resolving `package.json` literally would pin it and let the first `add_cell`/`edit_cell` persist nbformat JSON over the user's file; a MISSING `.py` is an error, never a rewrite, because Cellar cannot CREATE one - `jpFormat` is read from the file, so an invented doc persists nbformat JSON into a `.py`. `list_notebooks` and the open path share ONE marker rule (`jupytext.ts`'s `isPyNotebookFile`, a bounded 64 KB header read - it sniffs every `.py` in the workspace on the shared event loop, off ONE module-level buffer rather than a fresh allocation per file, and `detectPyNotebook` reads its first non-blank line by WALKING the leading lines instead of splitting the whole prefix into an array to read one - so a plain module is neither listed nor openable and everything listed can be pinned). That bound applies the UI detection route's marker VOCABULARY to a header prefix rather than the whole file and is deliberately **not** a parity claim against it: the two disagree at the extremes in both directions (a `# %%` past 64 KB, a `.py` past the UI's own `MAX_FILE_BYTES` read cap). Because a `.py` cannot HOLD a code root, `use_notebook`'s handler raises **that** refusal up front too (`assertRootUsable(root, name)`, the write path's own `textNotebookRootError`), beside the directory resolve and for the same reason - the open-or-create and the pin are irreversible by the time `setNotebookRoot` would throw, so `use_notebook('x.py', root)` used to leave the session silently pinned behind its own error. The agent-facing descriptions and INSTRUCTIONS clause 6 say a `.py` is listed and pinned AS-IS, since `create_if_missing` defaults to true and stripping the extension MINTS and pins a stray empty `.ipynb`. Covered by `tests/unit/mcp-py-notebook.test.ts` + the `.py`-root ordering cases in `tests/unit/mcp-notebook-root.test.ts`. Covered by `tests/unit/mcp-session-notebook.test.ts`.
- **The agent surface reports user-initiated CHANGE, not only STATE (`src/lib/server/mcp/userActivity.ts`).** Every read tool is a snapshot of "now", so when the human deleted a cell in the UI all ~7 id-addressed tools answered with the SAME bare `no cell matches id "..."` string a typo gets - indistinguishable from a hallucinated handle, so the model concluded "the tool is flaky / handles don't round-trip" (which is what the watching user reads as a broken tool) - and a user EDIT/ADD/MOVE was absorbed in total silence, the tool returning the rewritten notebook as if the agent had always known it. Two halves over ONE event tap. **(B) Tombstones**: a bounded per-notebook registry of recent deletions (`cellId -> {at, by, origin}`, cap 200, `TOMBSTONE_TTL_MS` 30min) consulted at the SINGLE resolve chokepoint `resolveOne`/`resolveMany` (`server.ts`), so a recently-deleted handle names the CAUSE ("the user deleted it just now in the Cellar UI ... the tool is working"). **Distinguishing recently-deleted from never-existed IS the feature** - an unknown id keeps today's generic message, and an expired tombstone correctly falls back to it. Still `isError` (the op really did not happen); only the text changed. The prefix scan is **floored at `MIN_HANDLE`**: it runs over up to 200 UUIDs, so a 1-3 char hallucinated ref hits one by pure coincidence and would be reported as a deletion that never happened - the "always claims a deletion" degeneration that destroys the very distinction above (no legitimate ref is refused; every handle Cellar emits is >=8 chars and a full UUID is 36). Attribution is **THREE-WAY**, decided by the tombstone's `by` against the CALLER's session - which is why `resolveOne`/`resolveMany` take the caller's `sessionId` as a **required** parameter (a site that omitted it would silently keep the coarser wording for that one tool): `by === caller` ⇒ **"you deleted it ... earlier in this same session"**, the commonest not-found path there is (an agent re-referencing a cell it removed while working from a stale plan), and it must NOT say or imply anyone else touched the notebook - describing self-inflicted staleness as a concurrent third-party editor pushes the model into re-planning around an editor that does not exist; a DIFFERENT session ⇒ "an agent deleted it"; `by == null` ⇒ "the user deleted it" (**never keyed off `origin`** - the navbar Consolidate-imports sweep deletes through a route that threads no `originId`, so that reported a human's own click as an agent's), with the " in the Cellar UI" clause alone gated on `origin`, since only a threaded tab establishes one. The lookup runs ONLY on a genuine not-found, which is why `cellHandle.ts` now throws a typed **`CellRefError`** carrying `code: 'empty'|'ambiguous'|'not_found'` (messages byte-identical): an `ambiguous` ref names a LIVE collision a deletion note would misdescribe, and matching on message text would be brittle. **(A) Digest**: `wrapToolsWithUserActivity` wraps `server.registerTool` ONCE inside `registerTools`, so all ~51 tools **and every tool added later** get it with zero per-tool edits; it appends an optional SECOND text content block (never a merged JSON field - that way it works uniformly for `text`/`notFound`/`textWithImages` with no re-serializing, and cannot be mistaken for notebook content), **omitted entirely when there is nothing to say, so an idle user costs 0 tokens**. The per-session cursor is `Map<sessionId, Map<nb, seq>>` over `events.ts`'s per-notebook monotonic `seq` (newly exported as **`currentSeq(nb)`**), snapshotted **BEFORE** the handler runs so a note can never describe the very call returning it, advanced **unconditionally** past filtered-out events (else a non-reportable change re-surfaces later), and **seeded silently on a session's FIRST call** for a notebook (there is no "last call" to be since, and replaying the notebook's whole history would be enormous). Cleared in `service.ts`'s `forgetSession` via `forgetSessionActivity`, whose contract already mandated it. The 200-entry activity ring **TAIL-COLLAPSES**: a new entry whose `(cellId, kind, by)` triple matches the list's LAST element is dropped rather than appended (never a backwards scan, and never a `restored` - it carries no cellId and each restore genuinely invalidates the agent's handles afresh). Without it a human typing emits one `cell:edited` per 500ms autosave, so a burst on ONE cell fills every slot and evicts the earlier `cell:deleted`/`cell:added`/`cell:moved` the agent most needed. The collapsed entry **KEEPS ITS ORIGINAL `seq`** - do not "fix" that by moving it forward. **Stated residual:** once a cell's edit has been reported to a session, further CONSECUTIVE edits of that same cell - with no other reportable change in between - are not re-reported to it, since the entry's seq stays below its cursor. That is the deliberate trade against advancing the seq, which would instead hide the change from a session whose cursor lands BETWEEN the two seqs; tail-ness alone does not prove none does, because the notebook's seq counter is bumped by EVERY published event, not only reportable ones. It is a bounded, stated trade, not an invariant, and it is consistent with `summarize`'s own one-verb-per-cell PRECEDENCE rule, applied across calls rather than only within one.
	- **Excluding the agent's OWN actions is done with an `AsyncLocalStorage`, NOT with `originId` alone.** `runAsAgent(sessionId, fn)` tags each tool handler; the rule is "report every change whose origin is not MINE", which covers the human **and** a second agent (an originId-only rule would silently hide another agent's edits, since MCP mutations carry no originId). It is read **in the bus listener, not written onto the event**: `EventEmitter.emit` is synchronous, so `publish()` inside a handler runs the listener in that handler's async context - which is why the SSE wire stays byte-identical, and that matters because the same bus carries streamed output deltas at ~40ms and pays per byte. Work escaping the context reads as foreign, i.e. over-reported rather than silently attributed to the agent. **The cost is stated in the module so that argument does not read one-sided:** the first `runAsAgent` call enables ALS, which turns on async-hooks / promise-hook instrumentation for the WHOLE Node process for the rest of its lifetime - not only MCP handlers - and that process also carries the kernel websockets, the ~40ms delta fan-out and every SSE stream; on Node 20 (the pinned CI LTS, which predates AsyncContextFrame) that has a real promise-path cost. It is UNMEASURED - a known unquantified cost, not a claim that it is negligible.
	- **`hidden_from_agent` is honored in both halves, through the ONE shared predicate `src/lib/agentVisibility.ts` (`isHiddenFromAgent`) - never a second copy.** `service.ts` and `userActivity.ts` each held a verbatim `metadata?.cellar?.hidden_from_agent === true`, and they cannot import each other (`service.ts` already imports `userActivity.ts` for `forgetSessionActivity`), so the two could drift silently - and a digest reading the flag differently from the read tools would NAME a cell those tools deliberately refuse to show. That is a disclosure bug, not a style question, hence a pure browser-safe module on the `hideInput.ts` precedent. Non-delete kinds are checked against the LIVE document at digest time (the honest moment); a DELETED cell cannot be, since `cell:deleted` fires after the splice - so `notebook.ts`'s `deleteCells` captures the flag before removal and carries **`hiddenFromAgent: true`** on the event (only ever when true, so the ordinary event is unchanged), and such a cell gets NO tombstone and NO digest entry. General rule in the summarizer: **a cell whose visibility cannot be verified is not reported.** That rule reaches the ATTRIBUTION too: `actorPhrase` is fed the entries that actually produced the sentence (the restore entries for the restore clause, the entries whose cell survived the bucket filter otherwise), **never the unfiltered window** - saying "another agent" when the only thing that agent touched was a filtered-out cell both credits it with a cell it never touched and discloses that the hidden cell changed at all, which is the invariant the filter exists for. It is a NARROWING of the input, not of the rule: a reported cell touched by both still yields "the user and another agent".
	- **The `INSTRUCTIONS` pre-announcement is LOAD-BEARING, not polish.** Probed against a real model: an UNANNOUNCED "the user deleted a cell" note reads as PROMPT INJECTION and is refused; with the channel declared up front the identical payload makes the model attribute the change to the human and adapt. The clause is a paragraph before "Follow this house style" and INTERPOLATES `DIGEST_PREFIX` so prose and payload cannot drift (the `IMAGE_DOC` precedent). Do not drop it as redundant. **Its ACCEPTED cost - do not re-raise it as a bug:** `DIGEST_PREFIX` is a fixed, UNAUTHENTICATED literal that this very clause declares trustworthy, so a shared/downloaded notebook whose CELL OUTPUT prints that literal can forge what reads as a trusted Cellar note (output text is JSON-stringified into the payload block and the digest is appended to the SAME content array, so both reach the model as text). The surface exists BECAUSE of the clause - the clause is what makes the marker trusted - i.e. it is the mirror image of the injection problem the clause solves. Weighed and ACCEPTED by the user; the bound is recorded in `userActivity.ts`'s header alongside the other two, with the mitigation named should it ever matter (a per-session random token in the marker, interpolated into INSTRUCTIONS exactly the way `DIGEST_PREFIX` already is).
	- **PUSH CANNOT DO THIS - do not build it.** Probed against Claude Code 2.1.220: the client never sends `resources/subscribe`, and `notifications/message` / `resources/updated` / `resources/list_changed` were all delivered and NONE reached the model. Pull on the agent's own calls is the only channel. Reported kinds (v1) are delete/add/edit/move/cell-type + `notebook:restored` (a wholesale revert, reported alone since prior handles may be meaningless); deliberately NOT outputs-cleared (visible as `has_output:false`), display/role/export settings, or runs. Design + evidence: `firstmate/data/cellar-agent-sees-user-actions-u3/report.md`. Covered by `tests/unit/mcp-user-activity.test.ts` (module behavior + an in-memory MCP client over the REAL `registerTools`) and `tests/e2e/mcp-user-activity.spec.ts` (real launcher + real `cellar mcp` bridge + the real REST routes - the only level that proves `extra.sessionId` really arrives from `StreamableHTTPServerTransport`, without which the whole feature would be silently inert).
- **Zero-config agent connection (`cellar mcp`).** Because the MCP HTTP port is per-run dynamic, agents are pointed at the stdio command `cellar mcp`, never a URL. On launch `bin/cellar.js` writes `<ws>/.cellar/runtime.json` (`{mcpPort,appPort,jupyterPort,pid}`, ephemeral - gitignored, cleared in `cleanup()` on shutdown) and writes/merges `<ws>/.mcp.json` with a `cellar` stdio entry (`--no-mcp-config` opts out). `runtime.json` lives in `src/lib/server/runtime.js` (node-builtins + `fetch` only, importable by launcher and `$lib`); the `.mcp.json` merge lives entirely in `src/lib/server/harness.js` (see the next entry) - `runtime.js` writes no agent config - and is idempotent and preserves other servers. The `mcp` subcommand (handled at the top of `bin/cellar.js`, before normal arg parsing) calls `runMcpBridge` in `src/lib/server/mcp-bridge.js`: reads runtime.json, verifies alive (pid AND a GET to `/mcp`), then transparently proxies JSON-RPC both ways between a `StdioServerTransport` and a `StreamableHTTPClientTransport` to the live server (schema-agnostic, so it never drifts). Missing/dead instance → exit 1 with a stderr message that names the CAUSE (nothing is running here, the bridge never starts one) rather than reading as a misconfiguration, and prints the `cd <ws> && cellar` that fixes it - deliberately still a message, NOT an auto-launch (booting a headless second Cellar behind the user's back is a separate decision). `runMcpBridge` returns a promise that stays pending until shutdown - the launcher must `await` it (an early `process.exit` kills the bridge right after connect). Both new modules are added to `package.json` `files`. The sidebar "Connect an agent" panel is reframed around this flow (see Sidebar entry below).
- **Harness MCP setup (`src/lib/server/harness.js`) - one registry, an ALLOW-LIST reconciled every start, two entry points.** Every agent harness needs the SAME stdio server (`cellar mcp`) in a DIFFERENT file, and the difference is the whole reason this exists: **Codex does not read `.mcp.json`** (it reads a project `.codex/config.toml` `[mcp_servers.cellar]` table, and only for a project the user has TRUSTED), so Cellar's zero-config `.mcp.json` was silently invisible to it. `HARNESSES` maps each supported harness to its workspace-relative config path + format (`claude` → `.mcp.json` JSON; `codex` → `.codex/config.toml` TOML - the exact shape `codex mcp add` itself writes, verified against a real Codex CLI); **adding a harness is a DATA addition, never new control flow**, and it reaches both entry points at once. Node builtins only (no TOML dependency), in `package.json` `files`, so `bin/cellar.js` imports it like `venv.js`/`runtime.js` - and it is the ONLY writer of an agent config, so the automatic per-launch write and an explicit `cellar harness add claude` are one code path (`runtime.js` used to carry `writeMcpConfig`/`mcpConfigPath` aliases; they were deleted once every caller moved here, rather than kept as a second name for the same rule). **The durable state is an ALLOW-LIST of harnesses Cellar may configure here, and the launch RECONCILES it on every start** (`reconcileHarnesses` → `configureHarness` per allowed harness): each one's config is checked and, if it is missing, stale or was deleted, repaired. That is what makes the list a standing instruction rather than a one-off write - delete `.mcp.json` and the next `cellar` puts it back. **It replaced a DECLINE marker, and the reason is the whole design:** a decline model made the most reflexive answer the most destructive one - the prompt runs before the per-launch write, so on a new workspace Claude Code always read "not configured", and pressing Enter recorded a decline that permanently switched off the zero-config `.mcp.json` write. An allow-list inverts that: nothing is ever declined, a harness is either on the list or not yet on it, and **no answer - or absence of one - can take capability away**. Claude Code is on the list BY DEFAULT (`defaultAllowed`), so a workspace with no marker behaves exactly as it always has, with no migration. Scope is per-workspace (the same `.cellar/harness.json`), deliberately not global: which agent you point at a project is a property of that project, and a fresh clone should get the defaults rather than inherit another machine's answer - the GLOBAL default lives in the registry as `defaultAllowed`, which **SEEDS** a workspace and does not override it: the first write freezes today's defaults into an explicit list, and from then on that list is AUTHORITATIVE, including against a harness a LATER release marks `defaultAllowed`. That is deliberate and is what an allow-list means - it may only grow by an explicit act, so upgrading Cellar must never silently begin writing a NEW config file into a project the user already answered for. The cost is discoverability, paid by `promptedFor` (below), never by unioning the defaults at read time. `readAllowList` falls back to the defaults ONLY when no list was ever written, which is why `disallowHarness` must persist an explicit (possibly empty) list - otherwise the fallback would silently re-add what `cellar harness remove claude` just took out. `--no-mcp-config` passes the harness owning that FILE as `exclude` (derived from the ONE registry helper **`mcpJsonHarnessNames()`** - `configPath === MCP_JSON_CONFIG_PATH`, never a pinned name), and an exclusion is per-launch only - never a change to the list. **Because it is per-LAUNCH, the app cannot infer it from the workspace marker**, so the launcher passes it through the app spawn env as `CELLAR_NO_MCP_CONFIG` and `+page.server.js` reports it as a THIRD fact (`projectRepairPaused`) beside `projectConfigured`/`projectManaged`: without it the sidebar banner claimed the every-start repair over a `.mcp.json` this instance deliberately leaves alone. The three are kept apart because their remedies differ - `cellar harness add claude` for an unmanaged entry, relaunching without the flag for a paused one - and `paused` is reported only for a MANAGED harness, since an unmanaged one is not repaired by any launch. **Writes MERGE and refuse rather than clobber** (these files hold the user's other MCP servers and settings): idempotent (`already` → zero bytes written), and anything it cannot edit confidently returns an actionable `skipped` - a file that cannot be READ at all (EACCES, or a directory where the config should be; both formats go through the ONE `readConfigText`, so neither throws a raw stack trace out of `cellar harness list`/`add` where only the JSON side used to be guarded), non-object JSON, a **non-object `mcpServers`** (the JSON sibling of the `mcp_servers = { … }` refusal below - the merge would otherwise swap the user's value for `{ cellar: … }` and report success), unreadable TOML, or a `cellar` server defined in another legal TOML form (inline table, dotted key), which is DETECTED so a duplicate is never appended. **The merge reaches INSIDE the `cellar` entry in BOTH writers, and idempotence is decided on MEANING, not on bytes.** Cellar owns `command`/`args` and nothing else, so a key the user added beside them (`env`/`type`/`cwd`) survives - the JSON writer spreads the existing entry under the canonical one exactly as the TOML writer rewrites only its two lines; replacing the entry wholesale dropped them. And an entry that already matches returns `already` and writes NOTHING whatever the file's formatting: comparing our re-serialized bytes against the user's meant a correct config indented differently was reported `updated` and rewritten on **every single launch**. The TOML writer is **text-surgical, deliberately not a parse/serialize round-trip**: it rewrites only the `command`/`args` lines inside the existing table (or appends the table), so comments, key order and every unrelated setting survive byte-for-byte - **per KEY, not merely per table**: a splice replaces whole physical lines, so a key already SAYING what Cellar would write is left alone (rewriting `command = "cellar"  # the stdio bridge` because its `args` sibling is wrong destroyed that comment to change nothing). What "already says it" means lives ONCE, in `TOML_KEYS`, which also builds the appended block and backs `tableMatches`, so the idempotence check and the writer cannot disagree. Its scanner is therefore the ONE place that decides what is STRUCTURE and what is a VALUE, and it must track BOTH continuations to do it: an open `"""` multi-line string (a `[table]`-lookalike in one is DATA, and reading it as a table reports an unconfigured harness as already done) **and an open bracket**. Every structural decision - the `[`/`]` depth of a value, the `=` that opens one - reads a STRING-MASKED copy of the line (`scanLine`'s `masked`, same length so indices align), never the raw text, and `parseTomlDoc` records each key's value span ONCE (`valueFrom`/`last`) so `readAssignment` reads it back instead of re-scanning. Getting either wrong does not look like a parse error, it looks like a config that quietly lost something: counting an in-string `[` ran a value's span to the end of the table and the rewrite **spliced the user's sibling keys away**; a `[1, 2]` array element read as a table header truncated the span, so a key that WAS present read as missing and a **duplicate** was inserted (invalid TOML); and re-scanning let a `command = "…"` line inside a `"""` block be rewritten while the real key kept its wrong value. Refusal covers the rest: a value whose brackets never close is `malformed`, and a `cellar` defined in another legal form is `skipped` - including `mcp_servers` itself assigned as a VALUE (`mcp_servers = { cellar = … }`), whose key path is a mere PREFIX of `mcp_servers.cellar`, because TOML forbids extending an inline table so appending the canonical one would leave the whole file unparseable. Both writers replace the file **atomically** (unique temp in the target's own dir → fsync → rename, `writeFileAtomic`), so a crash mid-write cannot truncate a config holding the user's other MCP servers and settings. Cellar deliberately does NOT write Codex's project TRUST (it lives in the user's global `~/.codex/config.toml` and widens the agent's sandbox - the user's call), so the result carries that note instead. **Entry point 1: the first-run prompt** (`maybePromptHarnessSetup` in `bin/cellar.js`, placed **before the single-instance takeover** and the slow toolchain work - the question is answered while the terminal is quiet, and, load-bearing, a relaunch must never reap the instance that owns this folder and THEN sit waiting for an answer, leaving the user with no running Cellar at all). Every gate is the pure, unit-tested `shouldPromptHarnessSetup`: asked ONCE PER HARNESS via a durable `.cellar/harness.json` marker (`promptedFor` records WHICH harnesses the question covered, so a harness registered by a later release is still presented once instead of inheriting an answer given before it existed; an OLDER marker carrying `promptedAt` but no `promptedFor` reads as having covered today's registry, so an upgrade never re-asks about what the user was already shown - at the stated cost that such a marker also silences the question for a harness registered after it was written, a gap bounded to markers predating the field), never without a TTY (`-y`/`$CI`/piped stdin all fall through), never offering a harness the launch would not write anyway (**`--no-mcp-config` passes the `auto` harness as `exclude`**, so the prompt cannot write the exact `.mcp.json` that flag refuses - it is OMITTED, never declined, since a decline would outlive the flag), and wrapped so it can NEVER abort a launch - **nor STALL or SUSPEND one, which takes TWO mechanisms because they cover different failures**. `ask` covers a stdin that is merely SILENT: it gives up on a close, a read error, or `HARNESS_PROMPT_TIMEOUT_MS` (30s), and its timer is deliberately **not** unref'd - the launch awaits that promise, so a timer the loop may ignore would let the process fall out from under the prompt instead of giving up and continuing. A **backgrounded `cellar &`** is a different failure and the timeout is NOT a substitute for it: it reads `stdin.isTTY` as true (so `autoYes` stays false), and reading the controlling terminal from a background process group raises SIGTTIN, whose default disposition **STOPS** the process - and a stopped process runs no timers, so no timeout can ever fire and the job sits suspended until `fg`. **Overriding that with a SIGTTIN listener is measurably WORSE, do not "fix" it that way**: with a handler installed the read returns EIO, libuv retries it, and the process spins at **100% CPU** without ever reaching the JS handler or the timer (measured on a real pty; the unhandled case sits in state `T` at 0% instead). The only fix is not to read at all, so the prompt is gated on **`inForegroundJob()`** (`ps -o pgid=,tpgid= -p <pid>`, the same `ps`-scan convention as `instances.js`): a **proven** background job (`=== false`) is skipped outright, records nothing, and launches normally. Unknown - no controlling terminal, a `ps` without `tpgid`, Windows (which has no such stop) - still prompts behind the timeout, because reading unknown as "background" would silently retire the prompt everywhere that `ps` differs. **The question can only ADD, and that is the invariant to preserve:** it offers only harnesses NOT already on the allow-list (an allowed one is already kept in place, so there is nothing to ask), and no path through it ever calls `disallowHarness`. So a bare Enter, an explicit `no`, a typo, a closed stdin, the timeout and a background job are all harmless and differ only in bookkeeping: a real answer (Enter included) records `promptedAt` + `promptedFor` and closes the question for the harnesses it showed; a NON-answer (`parseHarnessAnswer`'s `answered` - no answer at all, or one nothing in which resolved) records nothing, so the next interactive launch asks again. **Ctrl-C is the ONE answer that stops the launch, and it needs `ask`'s OWN `rl.on('SIGINT')` listener**: readline runs in terminal mode here (stdout is a TTY), which puts stdin in raw mode and SWALLOWS ^C, so the module-level `process.on('SIGINT')` never fires and the keystroke read as "no answer" - the launcher then went on to reap instances, boot the sidecar and open a browser, the opposite of what it means. The listener closes the interface (restoring the terminal) and hands off to the launcher's EXISTING `shutdown(0, …)`; it records nothing, and it does not weaken the never-abort rule, which is about the prompt failing on its OWN. **`parseHarnessAnswer` classifies a token into FOUR buckets, and only the last may be reported as unrecognized** - reporting a name the module DOES know as "unrecognized" asserts something false about a valid answer: a name/number on the offer is `chosen`; a registered harness already on the ALLOW-LIST comes back in `managed` (understood, nothing to write - the prompt names those one line above the question, so typing one is the natural reply, and it COUNTS as an answer, settling the question instead of reading "nothing recognized" and re-asking on every later launch); a registered harness this offer does not carry is `notOffered` (reachable today - `harness remove claude` leaves it registered, off the list and settled - answered by naming `cellar harness add <name>`, and deliberately NOT counted as an answer, since unlike `managed` it asks for something the prompt declined to offer and says nothing about what WAS offered, so closing on it would retire an open question); only a name that is no harness at all is `unknown`. **The affirmative set mirrors the already-generous decline set** (`y`/`yes` alongside `a`/`all`/`*`): the offer is usually a SINGLE harness, so the question reads as a yes/no and `y` is the most reflexive positive reply - landing it in `unknown` meant the user said yes, nothing was wired up, and the same question came back next launch. The prompt names what is already handled before asking ("Already set up and kept in place: Claude Code"), so the question visibly reads as additive. **`record` is separately false when the offer was FILTERED** (`--no-mcp-config` hid a harness that is not yet allowed): closing the question over a partial view would suppress it forever for a harness that launch never showed. **Entry point 2: `cellar harness list` / `add <name…|all>` / `remove <name…> [--strip]`** (handled at the top of `bin/cellar.js` before arg parsing, never boots a server; honors `--workspace`). `add` puts a harness on the allow-list AND configures it now; `remove` takes it off so Cellar stops reconciling it but LEAVES its config entry in place, because "stop managing this" and "delete this from my config" are different requests and only one edits the user's file - `--strip` is the opt-in that also removes the entry (`stripHarness`, which refuses in exactly the cases the writers refuse, and which removes the cellar table **plus every table NESTED under it** - `[mcp_servers.cellar.env]`, array-of-tables forms included - because an orphaned sub-table leaves a `cellar` server with an `env` and no `command` that `codexState` then reads as `other-form`, refusing every later `configureHarness('codex')` for good). **A removal also records that harness in `promptedFor`** (`disallowHarness`), because an explicit removal IS an answer about it: a `harness remove claude` run before the first interactive launch leaves an empty list and no `promptedAt`, so without it the next start offered to add back exactly what the user had just taken away. Recorded PER HARNESS, never by stamping `promptedAt`, which would close the question for every other harness too - including one never shown, the very gap `promptedFor` exists to close (hence `promptedHarnesses` reads a `promptedFor` list on its own, and falls back to "everything registered" only for an OLDER marker that carries `promptedAt` with no list). `list` shows BOTH facts per harness - managed here, and configured right now - because the gap between them is the interesting state: a managed harness whose config is missing is precisely what the next start repairs. Both surfaces print that the tools arrive only **while `cellar` is running in that workspace** - the config alone is inert, since `cellar mcp` bridges to a live instance (see the entry above). The registry refuses what it cannot edit, but the WRITE can still fail on the filesystem (a read-only workspace, ENOSPC, EACCES on `.codex/`), so `add` catches **per harness** and prints that in the same one-line `[cellar] …` shape: `add all` continues past one failure and the exit code still says something failed, instead of exiting on an unhandled exception. **Both write paths record the ALLOW-LIST BEFORE writing the config** (`allowHarness` then `configureHarness`, in the prompt loop and in `harness add`): the list is the standing instruction and the write is only its FIRST reconcile, so a filesystem failure must leave a MANAGED harness the next start repairs. Ordered the other way, a throw skipped `allowHarness` while the prompt's `markHarnessPrompted` still closed the question, and the user's explicit "wire up Codex" was lost for good - nothing recorded, so no reconcile ever covered it. **All THREE `configureHarness` call sites catch per harness** - that verb, the launch-path write, and the PROMPT's own configure loop - for the same reason the prompt is wrapped: agent wiring may never abort a notebook launch. The prompt's is load-bearing beyond the outer catch, since a throw there escapes past `writeHarnessSetup`, discarding a successful sibling write AND the answer itself, so the question would come back on every later interactive launch. Three further write rules, all learned the hard way: the atomic replace **FOLLOWS a symlink** (`realpathSync` first, temp staged in the RESOLVED file's own directory), because rename installs a new inode over whatever it lands on - so staging beside the LINK replaced a dotfile-manager's symlinked `.mcp.json`/`.codex/config.toml` with a regular file and silently detached that setup; resolving first keeps the link AND the atomicity, and a target that does not exist yet resolves to nothing and is written in place. It **carries the target's MODE across, applied to the temp BEFORE the bytes go in** (temp+rename installs a new inode, so without it a `chmod 600` config - these routinely hold another server's `env` block with an API token - came back 0644; and because the temp sits in the TARGET's own directory, writing first left a complete copy of that config readable at the default `0o666 & ~umask` for the length of every write, so "set the mode on the finished file" - the obvious reading - is the wrong one, and the order is pinned by a source guard), and an INSERTED line **wears the file's own line ending** (emitting LF into a CRLF config left mixed endings, i.e. a two-line edit that diffs as the whole file; untouched lines keep their own bytes, so only new lines consult `dominantEol`). And a REFUSAL is not success: `cellar harness add` exits non-zero on `skipped`, or a scripted `add all` reported success having configured nothing. Covered by `tests/unit/harness-registry.test.ts` (both writers byte-for-byte, merge safety, idempotence incl. equivalent TOML spellings, the refusals, the value-vs-structure traps in both directions - an in-string bracket, a nested array, a multi-line string holding a lookalike key, the inline-`mcp_servers` prefix - the allow-list's persistence in BOTH directions incl. removing the DEFAULT harness sticking, reconcile repairing a deleted/edited config and writing nothing when correct, `stripHarness` in both formats plus its refusals, the prompt-once / non-interactive / can-only-ADD rules with a source guard that no prompt path calls `disallowHarness`, an explicit removal not being offered back while a harness never shown still is, the four token classes - `y`/`yes` taking everything offered, an already-managed reply settling the question and writing nothing, a not-offered one leaving it open, and no REGISTERED name ever landing in `unknown` - the filtered-view `record` rule in both directions, a source-order guard that the prompt precedes the takeover, the three no-answer outcomes exercised against the SHIPPED `ask` over fake stdio (closed stdin, read error, timeout), the pure `foregroundFromPs` verdicts in all three directions plus a guard that the gate is `=== false` and sits above every write, mode preservation (plus a source guard that the temp is tightened BEFORE the bytes), a symlinked config surviving a write AS a symlink with the real file receiving the merge, the launcher passing `CELLAR_NO_MCP_CONFIG` through and deriving the flag's harness from `mcpJsonHarnessNames()`, and CRLF, an unreadable config refusing rather than throwing in BOTH formats, and the CLI verb over a real `bin/cellar.js` spawn - list showing both facts, add/remove/--strip, and a refusal exiting non-zero). Note `inForegroundJob()` guards the harness PROMPT only: the sibling `confirm()` (venv/ipykernel) still reads stdin, so a backgrounded `cellar &` can be stopped by SIGTTIN there - a known pre-existing gap, deliberately left alone because refusing to read would change what such a launch DOES.
- **Live server→client run feedback (SSE, P0+P1).** An open browser reflects *agent* (or other-tab) runs with no reload: running indicator + streaming outputs. `src/lib/server/events.js` = in-process `EventEmitter` pub/sub (`publish`/`subscribe`); it stamps each event with a per-notebook monotonic `seq`. `src/routes/api/events/+server.js` = one SSE stream per browser tab (`text/event-stream` `ReadableStream`, `hello` + 15s heartbeat + abort/cleanup) carrying events for **all** notebooks. `src/lib/events-client.js` = a **single shared `EventSource` per tab** (ref-counted; stays under the HTTP/1.1 ~6-conn cap) plus a per-tab `originId`; every `LiveNotebook` subscribes to it.
